Yeah, um… I guess what brought me in is that some nights, usually after work or if dinner’s been tense, I start eating and it’s like… the part of me that’s supposed to make decisions just kind of shuts off. Yeah… I think so. Not every time, but on bad nights, especially if I’m wound up or stressed, I’ll start with like a full plate of leftovers, and then somehow I’m still in the kitchen eating bread or whatever snacks are there, and then ice cream.

I mean, sometimes afterward I just kind of sit there and feel stupid about it, like, “Why did I do that?” But guilt feels like a big word, you know?\n\nIt’s more like… I get uncomfortable with myself. And then I’ll distract myself with something, like checking my phone or thinking about what shift I have the next day. Work schedules have been all over the place lately, and that kind of throws me off too. But, yeah, I’m not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any issues with your sex life lately?\n\nPatient: Um… sex life? I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed anything specific there.\n\nI mean, lately I’ve been more focused on work and getting through the day — customers have been kind of intense, and my schedule keeps changing, so by the time I get home I’m usually just tired and sort of checked out. But as far as, like, an actual issue with sex… I don’t think I have anything clear to say about that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any difficulty with sexual arousal or pleasure recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I haven’t really been paying attention to it in that specific way. Like, arousal or pleasure, I don’t know if I could say there’s been a problem.\n\nLately when I get home I’m usually just tired and thinking about work stuff, like whether I’m opening the next morning or closing, because they keep changing the schedule. So I guess I haven’t noticed anything clear there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat more when you're feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every time, but on bad nights, especially if I’m wound up or stressed, I’ll start with like a full plate of leftovers, and then somehow I’m still in the kitchen eating bread or whatever snacks are there, and then ice cream.\n\nIt’s weird because it happens fast, like within an hour or two, and it’s definitely more than I’d eat if someone was sitting there with me. I’d never make that kind of plate in front of somebody. I don’t know, the kitchen just becomes this place I keep wandering back to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in your interest in sex recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I can’t really say I’ve noticed a decrease exactly. It’s not something I’ve been tracking, I guess.\n\nI mean, lately my head’s been more on other things, like work schedules and just getting home and trying to unwind. Sometimes I’ll sit down and then realize I’ve been watching some random show for an hour without really paying attention. But as far as interest in sex specifically… I’m not sure I have a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating helps you feel better when you're stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure if it helps, exactly. In the moment it’s kind of distracting, like I’m doing something with my hands and not just sitting there thinking.\n\nBut then I’ll be thinking about dumb stuff too, like whether I bought enough groceries or if the guy on the late shift is going to call out again, because that happened last week and then the whole register schedule got messed up. So… I don’t know. Maybe it takes the edge off for a little bit, but I can’t say I’ve really noticed it making me feel better in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty becoming sexually aroused when you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I haven’t noticed a specific problem with that, I guess. It’s not like I’ve been keeping track.\n\nSometimes I’m just tired after work, and then I get home and there’s laundry or dishes or I’m thinking about whether I forgot to pick up something from the store… and then the night kind of goes. S049\n  name: Eating_Large_Amount\n  description: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n  manifestation: On bad nights I can eat a full plate of leftovers, then keep going through bread, snacks, and ice cream in the kitchen within an hour or two, way more than I’d ever eat if someone were sitting with me.\n- key: S050\n  name: Loss_of_Control\n  description: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n  manifestation: What brought me in is that some nights after work or a tense dinner, I start eating and it feels like the decision-making part of me shuts off, like I can see myself reaching for more food but I can’t make myself stop.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: The binge eating and inappropriate compensatory behaviors both occur, on average, at least once a week for 3 months\n  manifestation: For the last few months, it’s been happening most weeks that I have one of those What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess what brought me in is that some nights, usually after work or if dinner’s been tense, I start eating and it’s like… the part of me that’s supposed to make decisions just kind of shuts off. Like I can see myself reaching for more food, almost like I’m watching it happen, but I can’t really make myself stop.\n\nI mean, work’s been weird lately too — being on my feet all day, people arguing over coupons and prices, it sounds silly but it wears you down — and then I get home and something small can set the whole evening off. But the main thing is the eating. It doesn’t feel normal to me, and I’m embarrassed about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you're eating when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, at the start I’ll tell myself I’m just having a little more, but then it’s like something switches off. I can see myself going back for more food, opening things, eating more than I meant to, and I’m thinking, “Why am I doing this?” but I don’t really stop.\n\nIt’s usually after work, or if dinner was tense… which, I know, everyone has stressful dinners, and work is just work, people get upset over the smallest things at the register—but yeah, in those moments I don’t feel like I’m in control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if guilt is the exact word. I mean, sometimes afterward I just kind of sit there and feel stupid about it, like, “Why did I do that?” But guilt feels like a big word, you know?\n\nIt’s more like… I get uncomfortable with myself. And then I’ll distract myself with something, like checking my phone or thinking about what shift I have the next day. Work schedules have been all over the place lately, and that kind of throws me off too. But, yeah, I’m not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any issues with your sex life lately?\n\nPatient: Um… sex life? I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed anything specific there.\n\nI mean, lately I’ve been more focused on work and getting through the day — customers have been kind of intense, and my schedule keeps changing, so by the time I get home I’m usually just tired and sort of checked out. But as far as, like, an actual issue with sex… I don’t think I have anything clear to say about that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any difficulty with sexual arousal or pleasure recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I haven’t really been paying attention to it in that specific way. Like, arousal or pleasure, I don’t know if I could say there’s been a problem.\n\nLately when I get home I’m usually just tired and thinking about work stuff, like whether I’m opening the next morning or closing, because they keep changing the schedule. So I guess I haven’t noticed anything clear there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat more when you're feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every time, but on bad nights, especially if I’m wound up or stressed, I’ll start with like a full plate of leftovers, and then somehow I’m still in the kitchen eating bread or whatever snacks are there, and then ice cream.\n\nIt’s weird because it happens fast, like within an hour or two, and it’s definitely more than I’d eat if someone was sitting there with me. I’d never make that kind of plate in front of somebody. I don’t know, the kitchen just becomes this place I keep wandering back to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in your interest in sex recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I can’t really say I’ve noticed a decrease exactly. It’s not something I’ve been tracking, I guess.\n\nI mean, lately my head’s been more on other things, like work schedules and just getting home and trying to unwind. Sometimes I’ll sit down and then realize I’ve been watching some random show for an hour without really paying attention. But as far as interest in sex specifically… I’m not sure I have a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating helps you feel better when you're stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure if it helps, exactly. In the moment it’s kind of distracting, like I’m doing something with my hands and not just sitting there thinking.\n\nBut then I’ll be thinking about dumb stuff too, like whether I bought enough groceries or if the guy on the late shift is going to call out again, because that happened last week and then the whole register schedule got messed up. So… I don’t know. Maybe it takes the edge off for a little bit, but I can’t say I’ve really noticed it making me feel better in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty becoming sexually aroused when you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I haven’t noticed a specific problem with that, I guess. It’s not like I’ve been keeping track.\n\nSometimes I’m just tired after work, and then I get home and there’s laundry or dishes or I’m thinking about whether I forgot to pick up something from the store… and then the night kind of goes. So if you mean like, physically having trouble when I actually wanted to, I don’t know that I can point to that. I haven’t really noticed it as a clear thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're experiencing less pleasure during sex than you used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed that specifically. I mean, it’s hard to separate out sometimes because I’ll be thinking about other things, like if I need to switch shifts with someone or whether I left something in the fridge too long—small stuff, but it gets in my head.\n\nSo… less pleasure during sex than before? I don’t know that I could say yes. I haven’t paid close enough attention to know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat more when you're feeling emotional, like upset or sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every time I’m upset, but on bad nights I’ll start with, like, a full plate of leftovers, and then I’m still in the kitchen after that. I’ll grab bread, then some snacks, maybe ice cream if it’s there.\n\nIt’s weird because if somebody was sitting there with me, I’d never eat that much in front of them. But when I’m by myself it can turn into an hour or two of just… picking through things, way more than I meant to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your desire to have sex?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t been thinking about sex a lot lately, but I also get home and I’m kind of wiped out, and then I’ll start doing random stuff around the apartment—like I’ll notice the trash needs taking out, or I’ll sit down and somehow it’s an hour later.\n\nSo… decrease in desire? Maybe? I don’t know that I’ve noticed it as a clear change. It hasn’t been something I’ve really measured, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're not in control during sex?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure what you mean by “not in control” there. Like physically, or more like my mind wandering? Sometimes I’ll sit down and then realize I’ve been watching some random show for an hour without really paying attention. Sometimes I’ll sit down and then realize I’ve been watching some random show for an hour without really paying attention. Sometimes I’m just standing there in the kitchen, and then I remember I still need to switch the laundry or I start thinking about some customer from work who was rude earlier, and it all kind of blends together. So… maybe it distracts me for a bit, but I haven’t really figured out if it actually makes me feel better.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think eating is a way for you to deal with feeling upset or sad?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 42 / cashier / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… maybe? I don’t know if I’d put it that clearly. Like, when I’m upset I might end up eating, but whether I’m “dealing with it” or just sort of avoiding thinking about it… I’m not sure.

Sometimes it’s just that I get home from work and my head’s full of stuff, like if the store was busy or someone was short with me, and then I’m in the kitchen and I kind of lose track. But I can’t say I’ve sat there thinking, “This is how I handle sadness,” you know? It’s more messy than that.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F52.7\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess what brought me in is that some nights, usually after work or if dinner’s been tense, I start eating and it’s like… the part of me that’s supposed to make decisions just kind of shuts off. Like I can see myself reaching for more food, almost like I’m watching it happen, but I can’t really make myself stop.\n\nI mean, work’s been weird lately too — being on my feet all day, people arguing over coupons and prices, it sounds silly but it wears you down — and then I get home and something small can set the whole evening off. But the main thing is the eating. It doesn’t feel normal to me, and I’m embarrassed about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you're eating when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, at the start I’ll tell myself I’m just having a little more, but then it’s like something switches off. I can see myself going back for more food, opening things, eating more than I meant to, and I’m thinking, “Why am I doing this?” but I don’t really stop.\n\nIt’s usually after work, or if dinner was tense… which, I know, everyone has stressful dinners, and work is just work, people get upset over the smallest things at the register—but yeah, in those moments I don’t feel like I’m in control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if guilt is the exact word. I mean, sometimes afterward I just kind of sit there and feel stupid about it, like, “Why did I do that?” But guilt feels like a big word, you know?\n\nIt’s more like… I get uncomfortable with myself. And then I’ll distract myself with something, like checking my phone or thinking about what shift I have the next day. Work schedules have been all over the place lately, and that kind of throws me off too. But, yeah, I’m not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any issues with your sex life lately?\n\nPatient: Um… sex life? I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed anything specific there.\n\nI mean, lately I’ve been more focused on work and getting through the day — customers have been kind of intense, and my schedule keeps changing, so by the time I get home I’m usually just tired and sort of checked out. But as far as, like, an actual issue with sex… I don’t think I have anything clear to say about that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any difficulty with sexual arousal or pleasure recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I haven’t really been paying attention to it in that specific way. Like, arousal or pleasure, I don’t know if I could say there’s been a problem.\n\nLately when I get home I’m usually just tired and thinking about work stuff, like whether I’m opening the next morning or closing, because they keep changing the schedule. So I guess I haven’t noticed anything clear there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat more when you're feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every time, but on bad nights, especially if I’m wound up or stressed, I’ll start with like a full plate of leftovers, and then somehow I’m still in the kitchen eating bread or whatever snacks are there, and then ice cream.\n\nIt’s weird because it happens fast, like within an hour or two, and it’s definitely more than I’d eat if someone was sitting there with me. I’d never make that kind of plate in front of somebody. I don’t know, the kitchen just becomes this place I keep wandering back to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in your interest in sex recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I can’t really say I’ve noticed a decrease exactly. It’s not something I’ve been tracking, I guess.\n\nI mean, lately my head’s been more on other things, like work schedules and just getting home and trying to unwind. Sometimes I’ll sit down and then realize I’ve been watching some random show for an hour without really paying attention. But as far as interest in sex specifically… I’m not sure I have a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating helps you feel better when you're stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure if it helps, exactly. In the moment it’s kind of distracting, like I’m doing something with my hands and not just sitting there thinking.\n\nBut then I’ll be thinking about dumb stuff too, like whether I bought enough groceries or if the guy on the late shift is going to call out again, because that happened last week and then the whole register schedule got messed up. So… I don’t know. Maybe it takes the edge off for a little bit, but I can’t say I’ve really noticed it making me feel better in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty becoming sexually aroused when you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I haven’t noticed a specific problem with that, I guess. It’s not like I’ve been keeping track.\n\nSometimes I’m just tired after work, and then I get home and there’s laundry or dishes or I’m thinking about whether I forgot to pick up something from the store… and then the night kind of goes. So if you mean like, physically having trouble when I actually wanted to, I don’t know that I can point to that. I haven’t really noticed it as a clear thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're experiencing less pleasure during sex than you used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed that specifically. I mean, it’s hard to separate out sometimes because I’ll be thinking about other things, like if I need to switch shifts with someone or whether I left something in the fridge too long—small stuff, but it gets in my head.\n\nSo… less pleasure during sex than before? I don’t know that I could say yes. I haven’t paid close enough attention to know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat more when you're feeling emotional, like upset or sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not every time I’m upset, but on bad nights I’ll start with, like, a full plate of leftovers, and then I’m still in the kitchen after that. I’ll grab bread, then some snacks, maybe ice cream if it’s there.\n\nIt’s weird because if somebody was sitting there with me, I’d never eat that much in front of them. But when I’m by myself it can turn into an hour or two of just… picking through things, way more than I meant to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your desire to have sex?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t been thinking about sex a lot lately, but I also get home and I’m kind of wiped out, and then I’ll start doing random stuff around the apartment—like I’ll notice the trash needs taking out, or I’ll sit down and somehow it’s an hour later.\n\nSo… decrease in desire? Maybe? I don’t know that I’ve noticed it as a clear change. It hasn’t been something I’ve really measured, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're not in control during sex?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure what you mean by “not in control” there. Like physically, or more like my mind wandering? I don’t think I’ve noticed that, exactly.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’m distracted—like I’ll suddenly remember I didn’t pay a bill or I need to get laundry out before it smells weird, which is probably not the most romantic thing. But feeling out of control during sex… I don’t know, I wouldn’t say that’s something I’ve really experienced or thought about much.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any difficulty with orgasm during sex?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know that I’ve paid close attention to that, honestly. I mean, sex hasn’t been super at the front of my mind lately, and when it does happen I’m usually kind of half in my head about other stuff—like work, or whether I forgot to buy coffee, which is ridiculous but that’s where my brain goes.\n\nAs far as orgasm specifically… I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear problem. Nothing that made me think, “Oh, something’s wrong,” I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you enjoy sex less often than you used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve been keeping track of that exactly. Sometimes I enjoy it, sometimes I’m distracted or tired, and then my mind goes off somewhere stupid, like whether I left something in the fridge too long or if I need to stop at the store after work.\n\nSo… less often than before? Maybe, but I don’t know if I’d call it a big change. It’s just not something I’ve really been paying attention to in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having less pleasure during sex?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I guess sometimes I’m not fully present, so maybe it feels less… I don’t know, intense? But I don’t think I’ve noticed a definite pattern like, “I’m having less pleasure now.”\n\nIt’s more like my attention wanders. Like the other day I was thinking about whether I’d remembered to put gas in the car, and then I’m sort of not in the moment anymore. But pleasure specifically… I don’t know that I can say.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel better emotionally after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “better emotionally” is kind of hard to pin down. Sometimes right after, I’m mostly just… relieved that it’s over, I guess, but I don’t know if that’s the same as feeling better.\n\nIt’s not like I finish and suddenly feel calm and happy or anything. My mood’s usually kind of all over the place anyway, like I’ll be thinking about work or whether my drawer was off at the register, or if I need to call my brother back. So it’s hard for me to separate what’s from the eating and what’s just the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat more when you're feeling upset or sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, not every single time I’m sad, but on bad nights, if I’m upset, I can end up eating way more than I meant to.\n\nLike I’ll start with a full plate of leftovers, and then somehow I’m still in the kitchen, having bread or whatever snacks are around, and then ice cream. But I can’t say I’ve sat there thinking, “This is how I handle sadness,” you know? It’s more messy than that."
  }
]
